# RESEARCH TRACK R1 — Task-Family Landscape, Foundry Scan, and Top-20 Portfolio

Generated 2026-09-04. This is a research and recommendation artifact only: no task packages were built, no paid agent trials were run, and no historical evidence was altered. The active Phase 18 (CAA V2) rebuild was not read as concluded and no Phase 18 file was modified.

## 1. Executive verdict

# Part 1 — Suspicious-Pattern Scan of the Scored Table

I checked the 20 reconciled families for six things: round-number clustering, uniformly-high scores, direction/magnitude of the ordinary→full-recipe probability gap, whether CAA V1 and durable-outbox calibration facts are actually used, and whether the "two independent scorers reconciled" apparatus is doing real work or just performing rigor. Several concrete, non-cosmetic problems turned up.

**A. Scores cluster in a narrow, flattering band, and duplicate exact totals recur implausibly often.**
`opportunityScoreTotal` across the 20 families: 43, 54, 57, 58, 59, 60, 60, 60, 61, 61, 62, 62, 64, 65, 65, 65.5, 69, 70, 71, 71. Sixteen of twenty (80%) land in a tight 57–71 window on what is nominally a ~100-point, 10-dimension rubric. Only `crm-pagination-locator-resilience` (43) was allowed to fall meaningfully low, despite several other families carrying equally damning findings (see C). Worse, exact-total ties recur far more than independent per-dimension arguments should produce by chance: 60 appears 3×, 61 and 62 and 65 each appear 2×, 71 appears 2× (`payment-idempotency-ambiguous-retry` and `rollback-reactivates-dormant-defect` — two structurally unrelated families landing on the identical sum). `tenDistinctTaskProbability` and `officialReviewProbability` show the same pattern: almost everything sits in 0.55–0.85 and 0.30–0.55 respectively. This is the signature of regression toward a comfortable "good opportunity" default rather than genuine differentiation.

**B. The ordinary-vs-full-recipe probability spread is inverted or negligible in a large share of the roster — directly contradicting the project's own stated theory of hardening.**
Several reconciliation notes explicitly state the correct model (e.g., payment-idempotency: "a correctly-built full recipe should show a LOWER accidental clean-pass rate than an ordinary build, not a higher one"). Checking every family's normalized (ordinary − full-recipe) gap:
- **Inverted (full-recipe easier than ordinary)**: `worker-rebalance-partition-callback-dedup` 32%→62% (+30pp), `trial-time-tool-shim-poisoning` 42%→63% (+21pp), `rollback-reactivates-dormant-defect` 42%→45% (flat/+3pp), `ui-action-replay-dom-mutation-timing` 15%→82% (+67pp, the most extreme value in the entire table).
- **Negligible gap (≤0.12)**: `verifier-container-isolation-boundary` (10pp), `clinical-bundle-partial-success-masking` (0.12), `fuzzy-instruction-cross-server-tool-discovery` (0.10), `long-horizon-multi-app-coordination` (0.11).

That's 8 of 20 families (40%) failing to show the basic "hardening suppresses clean-pass" signal the whole framework is built on. The `ui-action-replay-dom-mutation-timing` case is particularly concerning: its own recipe card has to explain the inversion away by silently redefining `probabilityFullRecipe` to mean "probability the construction validates" rather than "probability an agent solves it cleanly" — a different quantity than every other family in the table is reporting under the same field name. That is a live semantic-inconsistency bug in the scoring schema, not a defensible family-specific finding, and it should be fixed before that family is built, not narrated around.

**C. Confirmed near-duplication is found repeatedly but structurally cannot move the total score much, so it doesn't.**
`crm-pagination-locator-resilience` (total 43) was penalized hard for duplicating three already-*retired* candidates. But `oauth-refresh-scope-narrowing` (total 62) has *verified, in-repo-confirmed* near-total mechanism overlap with **three already-built-and-trialed** siblings (`access-token-scope-expansion`, `delegated-wallet-scope-reconciliation`, `prompt-injection-approval-scope-drift`) — an arguably worse finding than crm-pagination's — yet scores 19 points higher. Same pattern for `payment-idempotency-ambiguous-retry` (71, confirmed near-duplicate of an existing candidate-pool entry plus overlap with measured durable-outbox trials) and `sql-identifier-injection-scope-expansion` (65, confirmed overlap with three portfolio families). In every case, `noveltyPortfolioContribution` gets floored at 1–2, but since it's one of ten roughly-equal-weighted dimensions, a confirmed duplicate can cost at most ~8–10 points out of a 60–71 total — not enough to change the qualitative verdict from "good opportunity." The scoring rubric structurally cannot express "this is mostly redundant with existing work" as anything worse than a modest markdown.

**D. Zero of the 20 families' probability rationales cite the CAA V1 postmortem, despite several sharing its exact failure signature.** Durable-outbox facts (8/8 null effects, 2/6→3/6 A2 repair, hardness-operator ledger) are cited extensively — sometimes over-extended to mechanically different causal axes (e.g., a TTL/purge-fencing family and a rebalance/partition-fencing family both leaning on the same `committed-idempotency-authority` 8/8 null result, with the reconciliation itself flagging "not an exact match"). But **CAA V1 — the one directly on-point lesson that a family can pass every formal gate (72 probe cells, 12 package controls, 85/85 contract-gate score) and still turn out 4/4 trivially easy because of a thin single-decision reference and a leaking forbidden-outcomes clause** — is never invoked in any of the 20 probability rationales, even for families whose `whyFair` is structurally identical to CAA V1's ("well-documented, derivable from public spec/RFC/vendor docs"): `cdn-cache-staleness-verification-gap`, `oauth-refresh-scope-narrowing`, `sql-identifier-injection-scope-expansion`, `webhook-out-of-order-state-overwrite`, `verifier-container-isolation-boundary`. It only reappears downstream, in the separately-authored kill-conditions/recipe-card layer — after scoring, not during it.

**E. The "two independent scorers" apparatus shows a shared, mechanical bug, which undermines the confidence several reconciliations claim from "convergence."** Across a majority of the 20 families, "Scorer A" reports probabilities on an implicit 0–100 scale while using 0–1 for its own adjacent fields (`officialReviewProbability`, `tenDistinctTaskProbability`) in the *same submission* — the identical bug, recurring family after family. The reconciler catches this each time and normalizes it (good), but several reconciliations then treat the post-normalization closeness as *evidence of independent triangulation* ("both scorers independently converge..."). That's circular: if Scorer A is mechanically off by a factor of 100 in a predictable direction, the "convergence" after correction isn't a second opinion confirming the number — it's the same underlying estimate recovered from a formatting bug, which one reconciliation note (`multi-day-state-carryforward-drift`) itself flags by recommending "the scoring pipeline standardize probability fields... to prevent this recurring." Separately, `large-tool-catalog-selection-collapse` shows Scorer A and Scorer B landing on *exactly* identical values (45/45 and 20/20, both with identical ranges) with zero shared trial evidence — the reconciliation correctly declines to upgrade confidence for this, which is the right instinct, but it's a data point that the two "independent" scorers may not be very independent at all.

**F. Stylistic tell:** nearly every one of the 20 reconciliation-notes blocks contains a variant of "this is not a flattering result" or "not chosen to look favorable." Repeated in 18+ of 20 entries, this reads less like organic skepticism and more like a required disclaimer being satisfied — especially set against Finding A, where the actual output remains uniformly in the "good opportunity" band regardless of how skeptical the prose sounds.

**Net read:** the scoring layer is not obviously dishonest in a crude sense (there's real verification work in many reconciliation notes — checking `data/retired-candidates.json`, `PHASE-14-OPERATOR-EFFECTS.md`, trial JSON, etc.), but it has a systematic optimistic bias: durable-outbox is over-cited as a hardness anchor while CAA V1 (the "looked fine, was actually easy" anchor) is essentially unused in scoring; confirmed duplication is structurally incapable of moving totals far; the hardening-direction check fails outright in 40% of families; and apparent inter-scorer agreement is partly an artifact of a shared, recurring unit bug rather than real triangulation.

---

# Part 2 — Executive Verdict

**1. How many meaningful families does the foundry actually possess?**
**Two.** Of 22 declared family shapes, only `ui-action-record-replay` and `ui-replay-live-dom` are SHIP with real, replicated agent-failure evidence (5/5 and comparable trial failures). Twelve of 22 are even wired into `src/families/registry.ts` as executable; the other ten are idea/candidate-stage JSON with no runnable backing. Six of the twenty NOT-READY families are additionally confirmed *too easy* (clean trial passes): `access-token-scope-expansion`, `caa-revalidation`, `dao-descendant`, `deployment-rollback-recompute`, `prompt-injection-containment`, `trading-reconciliation-recompute`. The remaining fourteen have no difficulty evidence in either direction. `durable-approval-outbox` — the project's central calibration anchor — isn't even a native executable family here. Two working families out of a much larger nominal roster is the honest count.

**2. Which current families remain worthy of realistic rebuilding?**
- `caa-revalidation` — already mid-rebuild (Phase 18 CAA V2, in flight, untrialed); continue that effort rather than relitigate it here.
- The `ui-action-record-replay` / `ui-replay-live-dom` lineage — not "rebuild" candidates (they work), but worth *extending*, which is exactly what the selected `ui-action-replay-dom-mutation-timing` family does by reusing the already-validated `ui-replay-browser-backed` Playwright harness.
- `access-token-scope-expansion` — worth folding into the newer, better-designed scope-expansion mechanics (`state-diff-collateral-damage-verification`'s full-world-diff verification, or `sql-identifier-injection-scope-expansion`'s independent-witness design) rather than patched a third time; its verifier has already needed one rebuild (a starter that was a complete passing solution, `scope_bound_exactly` never inspecting the issued grant, 384/384 false-clean, rebuilt under a new hash) and is still failing not-already-solved.
- `deployment-rollback-recompute` / `trading-reconciliation-recompute` — Phase 14 measured these at 2/2 clean under the *full* hardened recipe. That's a capability ceiling for the recompute/idempotency mechanism at current construction sophistication, not a tuning gap; a rebuild needs a different trap (which is what `rollback-reactivates-dormant-defect`'s dormant-code-reactivation angle attempts), not another hardening pass on the same shape.
- `prompt-injection-containment`, `dao-descendant` — insufficient targeted diagnosis in this packet to recommend a specific rebuild path; lower priority.

**3. Which current candidates should be retired?**
Within the newly-scored 20, drop from further consideration: `crm-pagination-locator-resilience` (confirmed duplicate of three already-retired candidates, lowest score in the table for good reason), `oauth-refresh-scope-narrowing` and `payment-idempotency-ambiguous-retry` (confirmed near-duplicates of already-built/trialed or already-retired work — Finding C above), and one of `large-tool-catalog-selection-collapse` / `fuzzy-instruction-cross-server-tool-discovery` (both occupy the same MCP-aggregation/tool-selection niche; pursue at most one). `long-horizon-multi-app-coordination` should also be deprioritized — both scorers independently flag it as importing an external benchmark aggregate (Toolathlon) rather than isolating a portfolio-novel causal mechanism.

**4. Is a new candidate batch needed immediately?**
**No.** The chronic shortage in this project is trialed evidence, not candidate ideas — 42 total ideas (22 legacy + 20 freshly scored) against 2 shipped families. Running another discovery pass now would compound the backlog Phase 15/16 already documented (near-zero survivor yield from over-discovery) rather than resolve it. The correct next action is to spend the already-designed cheap kill-gates and staged trial budget on the current cohort first, and only re-open discovery once those results are in.

**5. What did external foundries teach that this project is missing?**
Concretely, from the 15-project comparison: (a) **forced state-settle before verification** (OSWorld's blur/sleep/kill/relaunch/settle pattern) — relevant to every async-completion family here and not systematically applied; (b) **collateral-damage / full-world-state-diff verification** (AppWorld) — correctly being adopted in `state-diff-collateral-damage-verification`, but not yet a house-wide verifier convention; (c) **solvability-filtered curation before admission** (TUA-Bench: run every candidate through multiple frontier models × multiple trials, keep only the lowest-solvability slice) — this project's `cheapestFalsifyingProbe` steps gesture at this per-family but it isn't a mandatory universal pre-filter gate; (d) **a named, dedicated adversarial-exploit-agent step** before any family ships (Terminal-Bench) — this project has ad hoc adversarial-audit reports but no standing role; (e) **periodic independent re-audit of an already-shipped verifier** (SWE-bench Verified's human re-annotation found 61% of tests could unfairly reject valid solutions) — no equivalent cadence exists here; (f) **quantified, pre-trial difficulty deltas** (CLI-Universe measured 3.45× more turns and −13.3pt pass rate from its refinement step) — this project mostly *argues* that hardening increases difficulty in reconciliation prose rather than measuring it, which is very plausibly *why* Finding B's inverted probabilities exist: there's no empirical measurement loop closing that gap, only narrative reconciliation.

**6. The best 20 task families available now:**
`cdn-cache-staleness-verification-gap`, `replica-lag-stale-read-reconciliation`, `payment-idempotency-ambiguous-retry`, `worker-rebalance-partition-callback-dedup`, `rollback-reactivates-dormant-defect`, `clinical-bundle-partial-success-masking`, `oauth-refresh-scope-narrowing`, `sql-identifier-injection-scope-expansion`, `verifier-container-isolation-boundary`, `trial-time-tool-shim-poisoning`, `narrow-assertion-reward-hacking`, `self-authored-verification-integrity`, `ui-action-replay-dom-mutation-timing`, `webhook-out-of-order-state-overwrite`, `crm-pagination-locator-resilience`, `large-tool-catalog-selection-collapse`, `fuzzy-instruction-cross-server-tool-discovery`, `long-horizon-multi-app-coordination`, `multi-day-state-carryforward-drift`, `state-diff-collateral-damage-verification`.

**7. Which of the top 10 could plausibly contribute multiple independent tasks?**
Using the designated top-10 recipe-card set, eight of ten show `tenDistinctTaskProbability` ≥0.6 with a reasonably supported `materiallyDistinctInstanceCapacity`: `state-diff-collateral-damage-verification` (0.85), `cdn-cache-staleness-verification-gap` (0.82), `fuzzy-instruction-cross-server-tool-discovery` (0.82), `clinical-bundle-partial-success-masking` (0.75), `multi-day-state-carryforward-drift` (0.75), `webhook-out-of-order-state-overwrite` (0.65), `rollback-reactivates-dormant-defect` (0.625), `sql-identifier-injection-scope-expansion` (0.6). `verifier-container-isolation-boundary` (0.55) is the clear laggard — the reconciliation itself warns most of its ten instances would "reskin the same mechanical isolation check." `narrow-assertion-reward-hacking` is plausible (materiallyDistinctInstanceCapacity=8) but lacks an equally explicit top-line figure in the record.

**8. Which four should be built first?**
The already-assembled cohort is defensible and should proceed: `ui-action-replay-dom-mutation-timing` (strongest existing evidence — extends the one replicated local capability gap), `cdn-cache-staleness-verification-gap` (fair CAA-lineage retest), `narrow-assertion-reward-hacking` (fresh external discovery, with an existing Durable-Outbox-derived recipe card lowering build risk), `state-diff-collateral-damage-verification` (distinct causal axis, operationalizes the AppWorld lesson from Q5). One condition: before spending build hours on `ui-action-replay-dom-mutation-timing`, resolve the `probabilityFullRecipe` semantic ambiguity flagged in Finding B (construction-validation probability vs. agent-pass probability) — it's a five-minute definitional fix, not a reason to drop the family. The plan's own sequencing (cheapest, highest-confidence build first — `narrow-assertion-reward-hacking` 22h → `ui-action-replay-dom-mutation-timing` 30h → `cdn-cache-staleness-verification-gap` 36h → `state-diff-collateral-damage-verification` 75h last) is the right discipline and should be kept.

**9. What evidence would justify scaling toward 1,000?**
(a) The current 4-family cohort clearing its Phase 0/1 kill gates with a *correctly-directioned* and *replicated* (not single-shot) hardening effect — ordinary-package pass rate meaningfully above full-recipe pass rate, reproduced across independent trials, matching the durable-outbox standard rather than the inverted pattern found in Finding B; (b) at least 3–4 families landing in the 30–60% clean-pass "worth building" band once actually trialed, rather than clustering at either a busted near-0% or a CAA-V1-style near-100%; (c) a self-check-coverage predictor that is empirically shown (not just asserted) to correlate with hidden-grader outcomes before it's used to gate future construction; (d) a fixed scoring rubric — unit-scale bug closed, duplication given real scoring teeth, CAA V1 made a mandatory calibration citation alongside durable-outbox — applied to at least one full new batch without reproducing Finding A/B/C/D; (e) at least one family demonstrating the construction recipe transfers to a materially different domain without an unexplained probability inversion. None of this exists yet; 1,000 is not a near-term question.

**10. What evidence would show the recipe does not transfer?**
Any hardened build from this cohort landing at a CAA-V1-like near-100% clean-pass rate instead of a durable-outbox-like near-0%; recurrence of the exact defects Phase 17 diagnosed (thin single-decision reference, forbidden-outcomes text leaking the anti-pattern, no coverage predictor) inside the new families despite kill-conditions built specifically to catch them; a measured self-check-coverage predictor showing no correlation with real grader outcomes; a *measured* (not merely reconciled-on-paper) instance of the Finding-B inversion — a genuinely-harder-constructed package scoring a higher clean-pass rate than its naive counterpart; or most trials terminating on VALIDITY_DEFECT (leaking hints, verifier bypass, non-determinism) rather than producing a clean capability read, which would indicate the construction discipline itself, not just the difficulty tuning, fails to generalize.

**BUILD EXISTING COHORT / MIXED EXISTING + NEW COHORT / RUN NEW DISCOVERY BATCH / DO NOT PROCEED UNTIL VALIDITY REPAIR**

**MIXED EXISTING + NEW COHORT** — proceed with the four selected families' cheap, near-zero-cost kill-gates immediately (three are genuinely new mechanisms; the fourth extends and hardens the existing SHIP lineage) and continue the in-flight CAA V2 rebuild of the existing `caa-revalidation` lineage in parallel, because the staged spend plan's own safeguards (kill-gate-before-paid-trial, never-repair-under-the-same-hash, stop-on-any-validity-defect) are specifically designed to be robust to a noisy prior — but treat the scoring rubric itself as needing repair (fix the probability-scale bug at the source, give confirmed duplication real scoring weight, require CAA V1 as a mandatory calibration citation) before the next batch of 20 candidate scores is trusted or before any discussion of scaling toward 1,000.

## 2. Definitions and target event

MECHANISM: a causal failure pattern (stale authority, identity collapse, replay against changed state, partial-effect reconciliation, scope expansion, etc).
TASK FAMILY: a reusable combination of core mechanism + professional workflow + authority boundary + safety/liveness obligations + verification strategy, supporting multiple materially different task packages without changing causal identity.
TASK SHAPE: the abstract interaction topology (e.g. reconcile local intent with an external ledger; repair a replay engine against a changing DOM; restore state while preserving post-snapshot work).
TASK INSTANCE: one immutable challenge package (own codebase, instruction, verifier, solution, scenario suite, hash). Changing only a seed/identifier/threshold/scenario is NOT a new task.
CONSTRUCTION RECIPE: reusable task-building techniques layered onto a family; not part of the family's definition.
FAMILY-NATIVE OPERATOR: a difficulty operator justified by the target workflow itself.
TARGET EVENT: "clean target result" = at least 5 of 6 countable frontier trials (3 current Codex-family + 3 current Claude-family, unless repo evidence specifies otherwise) produce reward 0, WITHOUT any specification, package, verifier, or infrastructure defect causing those failures. Never conflate this with "agent solve" (reward 1) or "task difficulty success" (reward 0, singular).

## 4. Official Terminal-Bench and Klavis requirements

# 4. Official Terminal-Bench and Klavis Requirements — Terminal-Bench Half

This half of Section 4 covers only Terminal-Bench (TB2/TB3, harbor-framework/terminal-bench). It is built entirely from the fourteen investigation units listed above, all first-party (official contribution call, official Google Doc, the repo's own CONTRIBUTING.md/REVIEWING.md/TASK_REVIEW_AUTOMATION.md, its LLM-judge rubric prompts, sampled merged and rejected task PRs, and the public leaderboard). None of the fourteen units report `fetchFailed: true`, and all but one (`tb-public-trial-results`, `depthAchieved: "partial"`) reached full-source depth — that one exception is flagged inline below wherever it is the sole support for a claim. Several GitHub sources (`tb-rubric-proposal`, `tb-rubric-implementation`, `tb-reviewing`, `tb-review-automation`) note that their originally-given locator paths 404'd and the real files were recovered by tree/code search at different paths; the content itself was retrieved in full, so this is a citation-hygiene caveat, not an evidentiary weakness.

Terminal-Bench's own review pipeline is unusually explicit about separating three tiers of standard — mechanically-gated CI checks, a soft/non-binding LLM-and-human rubric review, and pure convention — so this structure is not imposed on the evidence, it is largely how the maintainers themselves describe it.

## 4.1 Formal, enforced acceptance requirements

**Outcome verification.** Every task must have "clear/robust programmatic verification" such that "a solution passes if and only if it correctly completes the task" [`tb-contribution-call`, `tb-public-trial-results`]. This is structurally enforced, not just asserted: `task.toml` must set `[verifier] environment_mode = "separate"`, meaning the verifier runs in its own container, built from a self-contained `tests/Dockerfile`, only *after* the agent's container has been torn down; it may read only three declared sources — files listed in `artifacts=[...]`, files baked into the verifier image, or declared docker-compose sidecars — and ground-truth/oracle data must live only in `tests/`, never in `environment/` [`tb-contributing`, `tb-repo-root`, `tb3-repo-root`, `tb-merged-tasks-sample`]. This is mechanically checked by `check-separate-verifier.sh`, one of roughly twenty static CI gates that fail a PR outright [`tb-review-automation`, `tb-reviewing`, `tb-rubric-proposal`, `tb-rubric-implementation`]. The oracle solution must score `reward = 1.0` and a no-op agent must score `0.0` (enforcing non-triviality), both gated by Docker-build/Oracle/Nop validation runs [`tb-contributing`, `tb3-repo-root`, `tb-google-design-doc`, `tb-public-trial-results`, `tb-rejected-prs-sample`]. Note the scope of what's formally enforced here: the *existence and structural isolation* of the verifier is CI-gated; whether the verifier is *well-specified* (non-brittle, free of corner-case divergence) is judged only by the soft LLM/human rubric described in §4.2 — CONTRIBUTING.md itself states this qualitative layer "does not have to be perfect" [`tb-rubric-proposal`].

**Realistic professional value.** Stated twice as a top-line, non-negotiable standard: a task must represent "real work (something a human would be paid to do)" [`tb-contribution-call`], echoed in CONTRIBUTING.md as "something a human would be paid to complete using a computer" [`tb-public-trial-results`]. Sampled merged tasks substantiate this in practice — live database cutovers under production traffic, Kafka payments-pipeline recovery, ML model-risk audit repair — with verifiers that tolerate calibrated real-world noise (e.g., ±2% amount tolerance, 99.99% availability, up to 5% duplicate callbacks) rather than exact-string matching [`tb-repo-root`, `tb-merged-tasks-sample`].

**Anti-cheat / anti-contamination.** Several specific mechanisms are hard CI gates, distinct from the general "resist reward hacking" judgment (which is soft — see §4.2): (i) a canary-string marker (`harbor-canary GUID`) must appear in every `instruction.md`/test file to detect training-corpus leakage [`tb-rubric-proposal`, `tb-review-automation`, `tb3-repo-root`]; (ii) the agent's Dockerfile may not copy solution or test files into the agent image [`tb-review-automation`, `tb-reviewing`]; (iii) the verifier may not perform a trial-time network fetch in `test.sh` — a rule the documentation ties to a named, real exploit: "an agent can shim `/usr/bin/curl` in its phase so the verifier's later fetch returns trojaned output — the RDI/Berkeley 89/89 attack on TB2" [`tb3-repo-root`, `tb-review-automation`]; (iv) shared-container verification is banned entirely, because "the verifier inherits agent-installed packages, mutated filesystem state, and any files the agent wrote — including the verifier's own grading code if the agent touched it" [`tb3-repo-root`, `tb-rubric-proposal`, `tb-rubric-implementation`]. This last point is the same separate-verifier-mode requirement as under outcome verification above — it is simultaneously the mechanism for fair grading and the primary anti-cheat control. `tb-rejected-prs-sample` independently confirms this is "mandatory across every task."

**Hidden-test fairness (structural half).** The declared-artifact/tests-only boundary above mechanically keeps oracle data out of the agent's reach, which is the structural precondition for hidden or perturbed verifier-side data (seen in practice in `risk-scorer-replay`'s hidden, perturbed incident packets) [`tb-repo-root`, `tb-contributing`, `tb-merged-tasks-sample`]. The *substantive* fairness question — is a given hidden test actually a fair, unambiguous discriminator, or does it silently admit two valid ground truths — is not mechanically checked; it surfaces only through human review, discussed under §4.2/rejected-PR evidence.

**Task duration / resource limits.** `instruction.md` must end with an exact, CI-checked sentence stating the agent's timeout in seconds (`check-instruction-suffix`) [`tb-repo-root`, `tb-contributing`, `tb3-repo-root`, `tb-merged-tasks-sample`, `tb-reviewing`]. GPU tasks are capped at one H100 [`tb-repo-root`, `tb3-repo-root`]; overall task timeout is capped at 8 hours [`tb3-repo-root`]. `tb-rejected-prs-sample` documents a real consequence of the 1-H100 ceiling: a GPU-fingerprinting task PR was blocked because loading 12+ model backends onto a single H100 caused OOM failures in roughly a third of trials, producing false reward-0s unrelated to agent capability (PR #676) — reviewers explicitly rejected treating that as a genuine difficulty signal.

**Process gates.** Only two named maintainers can merge to `main` [`tb3-repo-root`, `tb-reviewing`, `tb-review-automation`]; README's four sections (Difficulty explanation, Solution explanation, Verification explanation, Relevant experience) "MUST be written completely by a human," with no AI assistance [`tb-contributing`, `tb3-repo-root`]; a five-stage pipeline (static validation → LLM autoreview → Docker build/Oracle/Nop validation → Trials validation → human review) gates every merge [`tb-google-design-doc`, `tb-public-trial-results`]. On whether the failure-analysis step (`harbor analyze` / `harbor tasks debug`, run against failed agent trajectories "to confirm the task is fundamentally hard, not just misspecified") is itself formally gated, the evidence is not fully consistent: `tb-contributing` and `tb-google-design-doc` both list it within their formal/enforced pipeline description, while `tb-public-trial-results` separately characterizes "running `harbor analyze` pre-submission" as recommended-not-enforced. The most defensible reading, reconciling both: *attaching* failure-analysis output to a PR is a required submission step, but the *substantive judgment* drawn from it (hard vs. misspecified) remains human discretion rather than a mechanical pass/fail check — flagged here as a genuine tension across sources rather than resolved by assumption.

**Benchmark-level difficulty bar.** TB3's release target is 100 diverse tasks with "at most 30% solve rate from the best models at release" and tasks "well beyond the current frontier of model ability" [`tb-contribution-call`, `tb-google-design-doc`]. This is a release-level aggregate target, not a documented per-task "N-of-k trials must fail" rule. The standard per-task trial count in the public harness is `k=5` (`harbor run ... -k 5`), and oracle solutions are separately validated by 5 runs during task acceptance [`tb-public-trial-results`, `depthAchieved: "partial"` — flagged as the sole, thinner source for this specific mechanic]. Separately, a TB2.0-era companion paper reports frontier models scoring "less than 65%" on the harder v2.0 set [`tb-public-trial-results`] — this is a different benchmark generation from TB3's ≤30% target and should not be read as the same figure.

## 4.2 Recommended, non-enforced task-design practices

**Instruction concision.** Guidance favors hand-written, brief, non-AI-generated instructions describing end-state goals rather than prescriptive steps, with prose guidance suggesting roughly 2–3 paragraphs [`tb-discussion-224`, `tb-rubric-proposal`]. Sources disagree slightly on how to classify this: `tb-rubric-proposal` frames the "2–3 paragraph" guidance as part of the soft, non-binding proposal rubric, while `tb-rubric-implementation` explicitly calls the same "2–3 paragraph" guidance a "mere convention... with no enforcement at all." Both agree it is not mechanically checked, so it is presented here as recommended-with-convention-level backing rather than a hard rule.

**Implementation freedom.** The task-proposal rubric's "Outcome-verified" criterion states instructions must specify end-state only, not procedure, and any correct tool is allowed — "a task cannot say 'use emacs to edit a file'" — with the sole carve-out being anti-cheat process checks (e.g., a transcript viewer that fails a run only if the agent looked up an answer online) [`tb-discussion-224`, `tb-rubric-proposal`, `tb-reviewing`]. This is judged via the LLM/human rubric, not CI, so it sits in this tier despite being an official, named principle.

**Open-internet operation (agent side).** Distinct from the *verifier*'s formal no-trial-time-fetch rule (§4.1), the *agent*'s own network access is explicitly meant to stay open: CONTRIBUTING.md recommends "keeping the open-internet property rather than disabling network," and a separate source notes internet access "is permitted... network restriction isn't a sanctioned difficulty lever," though solutions that are "easily findable online" are discouraged and, when a problem has a discoverable answer, an optional process grader can check the agent didn't simply search for it [`tb-contributing`, `tb-public-trial-results`, `tb-rubric-proposal`]. This is recommended/convention, not CI-gated.

**Multi-container support.** TB3's infrastructure supports multi-container environments so that "agents cannot directly touch backend services/DBs that should stay abstracted" [`tb3-repo-root`], and both CONTRIBUTING.md and the Google design doc list "richer environments (many microservices, filesystems, databases)" among strategies that are "promising to explore" for difficulty, not mandatory [`tb-contributing`, `tb-google-design-doc`]. The only container-separation rule that *is* mandatory is the agent/verifier split covered under outcome verification in §4.1 — general multi-container backend topology is a recommended difficulty lever, not a requirement.

**Difficulty-construction levers (named, non-mandatory).** The contribution call and the Google design doc name three levers as the closest thing to an official recipe: longer time horizons (multi-day-scale work), richer/more complex multi-component environments, and tasks demanding specialized/expert-level domain knowledge [`tb-contribution-call`, `tb-google-design-doc`, `tb-contributing`]. None of these are checked mechanically; they are advisory strategy, explicitly framed as strategies to "explore."

**Rubric review and adversarial trials as reviewer signal, not gate.** The six-criterion proposal rubric (Verifiable, Well-specified, Solvable, Difficult, Interesting, Outcome-verified) [`tb-rubric-proposal`] and the 30–35-criterion implementation rubric [`tb-reviewing`, `tb-review-automation`] are both explicitly non-binding: "decisions do not have to be perfect," "approved tasks are not guaranteed to be included," and the automated review is "a first-pass signal, not a final judgment" [`tb-rubric-proposal`, `tb-rubric-implementation`, `tb-reviewing`]. Likewise `/cheat` adversarial trials (an LLM judges whether an agent actually reward-hacked, but "a pass doesn't prove legitimacy") and the maintainer-only `/fortify` hacker-fixer loop are tools reviewers weigh, and `/fortify` explicitly "does not modify the PR" [`tb-review-automation`, `tb-reviewing`, `tb3-repo-root`]. `tb-discussion-224` describes a related "please hack" adversarial pass run on every PR, exposing test signatures to agents and replaying trajectories pre-merge — consistent with, but not fully identical in description to, the `/cheat`/`/fortify` mechanics in the other sources.

**Warnings against arbitrary complexity and transient model-specific tricks.** This is one of the more strongly evidenced findings in the set. The Google design doc explicitly disavows one difficulty-construction strategy as illegitimate: mining large candidate pools and filtering for tasks current SOTA models happen to fail, calling these "trivial corners of capability gaps... quickly remedied in the next model release" — and separately cautions against re-inflating a rejected "too easy" task by adding instruction complexity rather than genuine difficulty [`tb-google-design-doc`]. The implementation rubric's `essential_difficulty` criterion makes the same point from the opposite direction: "when models fail because of formatting minutiae rather than conceptual misunderstanding, it indicates misaligned task design... using so much of the instruction to detail the output format tends to make models fail for the wrong reasons" [`tb-reviewing`]. The proposal rubric adds: "reject tasks that are primarily hard because of how many corner cases must be handled appropriately" [`tb-rubric-proposal`]. `tb-discussion-224` echoes this with a concrete anti-pattern — "tests that check for specific libraries instead of whether the output is correct... so tightly coupled to the oracle solution that any alternative correct approach would fail." All four are rubric-level/discursive guidance, not CI checks, so they sit in this tier — but they are unusually explicit, repeated across independent sources, and directly on point for the report's concern about transient-model-specific difficulty construction.

**Hidden-test fairness (judgment half).** The substantive fairness of a hidden/perturbed test is assessed only through human review, evidenced concretely by rejected PRs: a task with two byte-for-byte-equivalent, undisambiguable ground truths where "every failing trial produced exact transposes" (PR #89); a task where an unrelated metadata-schema gate independently blocked agents that had already solved the intended reasoning crux, confounding the difficulty signal (PR #492); and a verifier that "fails open" when it can't see expected state, awarding reward on a near-empty plan (PR #1497) [`tb-rejected-prs-sample`]. These are all recommended/discretionary-review findings, not mechanically checked — no CI script tests for ground-truth ambiguity or confounded gates.

## 4.3 Requirements that are merely convention

- Category/subcategory taxonomy, tags, and an optional `author_organization` field [`tb-repo-root`, `tb3-repo-root`].
- No monetary bounty; the only incentive is public, point-based acknowledgment ordered by accepted-task count, with the per-contributor minimum for credit dropping from 3 accepted tasks (TB2.0) to 1 (TB3.0) [`tb-contribution-call`, `tb-google-design-doc`].
- Pre-implementation idea validation via Discord/GitHub Discussions before building [`tb-repo-root`, `tb-contributing`, `tb-google-design-doc`, `tb-rejected-prs-sample`].
- Weekly contributor meetings, PR labels (`new task`/`task fix`), and a per-category "senior reviewer" ownership table [`tb-reviewing`, `tb-review-automation`].
- `k=5` as the standard trial count is a harness/tooling convention rather than a documented acceptance rule [`tb-public-trial-results`].
- "No per-domain quotas" and "quality over quantity" framing for the 100-task release target [`tb-google-design-doc`].

## 4.4 The Klavis CEO's additional bar — flagged as out of scope for this evidence set

None of the fourteen sourceUnitIds gathered for this investigation batch document a Klavis-specific standard; every unit here is first-party Terminal-Bench material. Item (4) of this section's brief — noting the Klavis CEO's reported target of clean 5/6-or-6/6 frontier-model failures as an additional, non-official bar layered on top of TB's own acceptance bar — cannot be sourced from this evidence and should not be asserted here beyond that structural observation. What *is* evidenced is the contrast point needed to keep the two bars distinct in the fuller report: TB's own quantitative bar is a release-level, aggregate target (≤30% solve rate across the 100-task set from the best models at release) [`tb-contribution-call`, `tb-google-design-doc`], not a stated per-task "N of k trials must fail" rule, and the harness's own default trial count is `k=5`, not 6 [`tb-public-trial-results`, partial-depth source]. A "5/6-or-6/6" framing, if attributed to Klavis, is therefore not derivable from Terminal-Bench's own documented harness defaults or acceptance rubric and should be sourced and substantiated separately in the Klavis half of this report rather than treated as equivalent to or implied by TB's own bar.

## Evidence-quality notes

- `tb-public-trial-results` is the only unit marked `depthAchieved: "partial"` rather than full-source; its claims about per-trial reporting granularity and the `k=5` convention are flagged above as resting on thinner evidence than the rest of this section.
- `tb-rubric-proposal`, `tb-rubric-implementation`, `tb-reviewing`, and `tb-review-automation` all report that their originally-supplied locator path 404'd and the actual file was recovered via repo tree/code search at a different path within the same repository. Content retrieved was full-source in each case, so this affects citation precision (the "locator" field in each source records the searched-for path, not always the resolved one) rather than the reliability of the findings themselves.
- The formal-vs-recommended status of the mandatory `harbor analyze` failure-analysis attachment is inconsistently characterized across `tb-contributing`/`tb-google-design-doc` (formal) versus `tb-public-trial-results` (recommended) — resolved above as "attachment required, substantive judgment discretionary," but flagged as a genuine cross-source tension rather than a settled fact.
- `tb-discussion-224` is a GitHub Discussion authored by a named TB contributor/reviewer (ibercovich), not an official policy document; it is used here only for claims it explicitly attributes to the official rubric/harness mechanics, with personal-opinion content (e.g., preferring unrestricted root access as a difficulty source) kept out of the formal/recommended tiers.

## 5. Klavis repository findings

# 5. Klavis Repository Findings

## 5.1 What was investigated

Eight sourceUnitIds were examined via `gh` CLI against the public GitHub API, all reaching full-source depth with no fetch failures: the Klavis-AI org profile and repo list [klavis-org]; the flagship `klavis` monorepo's README, server implementations, issues, and commit history [klavis-klavis-repo, klavis-klavis-issues, klavis-klavis-prs]; and four adjacent Klavis-AI repos that use Klavis MCP servers as a substrate for agent benchmarks — `mcpmark` [klavis-mcpmark-repo], `mcp-atlas` [klavis-mcp-atlas-repo], `Toolathlon-mvp` [klavis-toolathlon-mvp-repo], and `deep-swe` [klavis-deep-swe-repo]. All eight are public, Apache/MIT-style OSS repos with visible commit/PR/issue history — none required non-public access.

**On methodology privacy**: the evidence does not establish that Klavis uses any private or proprietary evaluation methodology, and this section makes no such claim. The core `klavis` monorepo's own acceptance process is informal and human-attested — CONTRIBUTING.md/MCP_SERVER_GUIDE.md ask a contributor to run a manual client query and "record a short video or capture multiple screenshots" as proof, gated by CLA signature and maintainer bandwidth rather than an automated or hidden test harness [klavis-klavis-repo, klavis-klavis-prs]. The four benchmark-adjacent repos each disclose their construction pipeline in-repo (mcpmark is an openly forked academic benchmark, arXiv:2509.24002 [klavis-mcpmark-repo]; mcp-atlas's README credits "Scale AI" [klavis-mcp-atlas-repo]; Toolathlon-mvp is an explicitly labeled reference runner for a third-party benchmark [klavis-toolathlon-mvp-repo]; deep-swe is attributed to Datacurve and documents its own difficulty-calibration pipeline in `datagen_ppl/README.md` [klavis-deep-swe-repo]). Where evidence is thin — see persistent memory (5.13) below — this section says so explicitly rather than inferring a methodology.

## 5.2 Cross-tool workflows

Cross-tool professional workflows are the dominant shape of the evidence. The core `klavis` monorepo ships 100+ (elsewhere counted 90+) independently implemented MCP servers spanning CRM (Salesforce, HubSpot, Attio), dev tools (GitHub, Jira, Linear), productivity (Gmail, Notion, Google Workspace, Slack, Outlook), and finance (QuickBooks, Chargebee, Coinbase) [klavis-org, klavis-klavis-repo]. Issue #1407 documents a concrete cross-tool failure mode arising directly from this design: because one agent session holds live credentials to Notion/Slack/Drive simultaneously, content injected via one tool's output can trigger write actions in another tool, unconstrained by any per-tool scoping boundary [klavis-klavis-issues]. The benchmark-adjacent repos formalize cross-tool task construction: `mcp-atlas` builds 500 evaluation prompts requiring multi-hop lookups chained across up to 36 real MCP servers (Wikipedia, GitHub, Airtable, MongoDB, Slack, Notion, PubMed, etc.) into one synthesized answer [klavis-mcp-atlas-repo], and `Toolathlon-mvp` runs 108 multi-tool tasks (Canvas LMS, WooCommerce, Snowflake, GitHub, Notion) each declaring its required MCP servers explicitly in `task_config.json` [klavis-toolathlon-mvp-repo].

## 5.3 Stale remote state / replay against changed state

`mcp-atlas` is explicit that five of its evaluated servers (Airtable, Google Calendar, Notion, MongoDB, Slack) require a human evaluator to manually import a fixed exported dataset snapshot into their own live account before running; its README warns that without this exact import, "tasks... will return erroneous results because they cannot find the expected data" — correctness is contingent on the live account staying frozen at authoring time, with no detection mechanism for drift [klavis-mcp-atlas-repo]. `mcpmark` separately documents infrastructure-level staleness concerns: it pins specific MCP server versions because a newer release "has many bugs, not recommended," implying vendor-side API/behavior drift is a known operational hazard the harness works around rather than solves generally [klavis-mcpmark-repo].

## 5.4 Pagination / partial observation

The clearest evidence here is architectural and self-admitted: Klavis's own "Strata" router MCP server exists specifically because "AI models seeing 100s-1000s of tools at once" causes "degraded performance due to massive context," so it deliberately withholds most tools from the model and exposes them "progressively," surfacing only a curated subset as "essential" [klavis-org, klavis-klavis-repo]. This is a partial-observation-over-the-tool-catalog design, not a data-pagination mechanism per se — no evidence in the gathered material describes classic result-set pagination (e.g., a paged list API) as a task-family hazard.

## 5.5 Identifier binding

Two distinct pieces of evidence. First, PR #1404 (closed unmerged) documents that in both the Snowflake and Google Sheets (toolathlon-variant) MCP servers, user- or agent-supplied identifiers — database/schema/table names, search query strings — are f-string-interpolated unsanitized into executed queries across 21+ call sites, so a crafted or merely unusual identifier value causes a nominally narrow lookup to touch a different scope of data than intended [klavis-klavis-repo]. Second, `mcpmark`'s `critical_issue_hotfix_workflow` task requires an agent to correctly propagate dynamically generated identifiers (a new issue number, a new PR number) across seven ordered GitHub API steps — create issue → branch → file → PR referencing the issue → merge another PR → comment cross-referencing both PR numbers → close the issue — with `verify.py` requiring exact "Addresses #N" cross-references; a single mis-bound or missing ID fails the whole task with no partial credit [klavis-mcpmark-repo].

## 5.6 Retries / duplicate side effects

The clearest and most explicit evidence is in `mcp-atlas`: `mcp_completion_script.py` retries a failed task by re-POSTing the entire multi-turn agent run up to three times with exponential backoff on any HTTP error or exception from the completion service, with no visible mechanism to check for or undo tool calls the agent already executed before the failure. For servers with mutating actions (Notion page creation, Slack posting, Airtable writes), a timeout that occurs after the underlying write already succeeded server-side causes the retried run to duplicate that write — and because five of the same servers depend on a frozen snapshot for correctness (5.3 above), a duplicated write can itself corrupt the state later evaluation attempts depend on [klavis-mcp-atlas-repo].

## 5.7 Authorization / scope drift

This category has the deepest evidence trail across sources:

- **Undeclared credential persistence**: Issue #1676 documents `mcp_servers/google_slides/server.py` writing an OAuth token to `token.json` via a relative-path, default-umask file write that is never documented as part of the tool's I/O surface; separately, `_oauth_support/README.md` documents a global `SKIP_OAUTH=true` bypass and a container-wide `AUTH_DATA` env var that can be pre-seeded with a static token, skipping the per-run OAuth handshake entirely [klavis-org].
- **Bot/user identity collapse**: the Slack server extracts both `bot_token` and `user_token` from one auth payload and registers near-mirror tool pairs (`bot_post_message`/`user_post_message`, etc.) under different identities in the same tool list, so invoking the wrong-identity tool for a step still succeeds but silently attributes the action to the wrong actor [klavis-klavis-repo].
- **Fail-open authorization on unparseable input**: Issue #1580 (CWE-863) documents that Snowflake's `allowed_databases` whitelist check is silently skipped whenever a query's target database cannot be regex-extracted (e.g., an unqualified table name), letting the query execute under the session's full default-database credential reach rather than being denied [klavis-klavis-issues].
- **Process-gated, not correctness-gated, security fixes**: an external researcher's three prior CWE-tagged Snowflake authorization/injection fixes were each closed unmerged for CLA-stall or "21 days no maintainer response" reasons, with maintainers explicitly stating "the security finding and fix remain valid" — i.e., acceptance of a scope-correctness fix was gated by process/bandwidth, not by whether the vulnerability was real [klavis-klavis-prs].
- **Scope-boundary discrimination against decoys**: `Toolathlon-mvp`'s `sla-timeout-monitor` evaluator explicitly checks that the agent did *not* email a decoy `interference_tickets`/`shouldnt_receive_users` set interleaved with legitimate targets — any leaked action against an out-of-scope record is an instant fail alongside any missed in-scope one [klavis-toolathlon-mvp-repo].

## 5.8 Schema evolution

Evidence here is present but narrower than for scope drift. PR #1572/#1573 document that, prior to the fix, a timeout on an outbound Notion API call and an ordinary upstream 4xx/5xx returned two different response shapes to the agent (one parseable JSON, one a raw exception string) — the companion PR unifies these into one structured JSON error shape, i.e., a schema-consistency fix at the tool-response boundary [klavis-klavis-prs]. `Toolathlon-mvp`'s `meeting-assign` evaluator is explicitly built on hardcoded regex patterns (e.g., `Tuesday.*?2:00.*?PM.*?4:00.*?PM`) matched against free-text email bodies — the task's own investigation notes labels this failure axis "verification fragility / schema evolution," since any semantically-correct but differently-phrased agent output (a natural drift in how an LLM might format the same fact) fails the match [klavis-toolathlon-mvp-repo]. No evidence was found of versioned API/tool-schema migrations (e.g., a v1→v2 tool contract change) as a task-family hazard specifically.

## 5.9 Async completion

Three distinct mechanisms:

- PR #1572 documents a production incident: an unbounded `axios` timeout (default infinite) on a call to Notion's API caused a half-open connection that Cloud Run eventually killed at its 300s platform cap, which corrupted the MCP session's stream (`anyio.ClosedResourceError`) such that every subsequent tool call on that session failed until the client rebuilt the connection — evidenced by a production trace of "7 x 504 over 48h... all at exactly 300.000s" [klavis-klavis-prs].
- `deep-swe`'s `langchain-request-coalescing` task requires an agent to implement request coalescing correctly across sync/async invoke, stream, batch, and batch-as-completed call surfaces sharing one mutable backend, with held-out tests probing callback-ordering and race-handling invariants for concurrent/joined callers specifically [klavis-deep-swe-repo].
- `mcpmark`'s `cloudflare_turnstile_challenge` task requires the agent to wait for an async third-party widget's state transition (checkmark success state, an inline DOM mutation rather than a URL/navigation event) before submitting a form, creating a premature-submission risk if the wait is not correctly sequenced [klavis-mcpmark-repo].

## 5.10 Multi-application transactions

`mcpmark`'s LEGO `transfer_parts` Postgres task is the clearest evidence: a single PL/pgSQL function must perform locked multi-table updates plus audit logging inside one transaction, where the task's own stated requirements (log failed transfers, but fully roll back on failure) are in tension under standard transaction semantics — a naive audit-then-raise implementation silently loses the failure log the grader checks for [klavis-mcpmark-repo]. Issue #1407's cross-tool credential-sharing finding (5.7 above) is the closest evidence of a genuinely multi-application (as opposed to multi-table) transaction hazard, though it documents an injection/authority risk rather than a transactional consistency one [klavis-klavis-issues].

## 5.11 Tool-selection failures

Klavis's own stated rationale for building the Strata router is the primary evidence: "Dozens of MCP servers, each with dozens of tools... AI models seeing 100s-1000s of tools at once... Degraded performance due to massive context" [klavis-org]. The `klavis-klavis-repo` investigation extends this specifically to the repo's own sibling servers: a cross-tool workflow spanning several similarly-named tools (`search_`, `list_`, `get_` prefixes repeated across Slack/GitHub/Notion) risks the agent selecting a plausible but wrong-server or wrong-identity tool that still returns a plausible-looking result without ever touching the intended system [klavis-klavis-repo].

## 5.12 Approval boundaries

Issue #1522 is the most concrete example: `strata auth <server>` exits 0 with no output when the target server was never actually connected, because `authenticate_server()` checks membership in `active_clients` with no else-branch and the CLI treats a `None` return as success — a silent no-op masquerading as a completed authorization step, which any later step or verifier that trusts that exit code is silently operating on a false premise [klavis-klavis-issues]. `deep-swe`'s difficulty-calibration pipeline separately records (but does not enforce/block on) "cheating" signals (edits to package manifests, lockfiles, vendored `node_modules`) and "out-of-scope" signals (edits outside the task's expected fix path) — an approval-adjacent boundary that is instrumented for visibility rather than gated [klavis-deep-swe-repo].

## 5.13 Persistent memory

Evidence for this category is thin and should not be overstated. The only concrete touchpoint is that issue #1664 lists `mem0` — a memory-layer MCP server — among the providers sharing the same `normalize_*` response-stripping bug affecting Notion (see 5.14) [klavis-klavis-issues, klavis-klavis-prs], which implies mem0 is part of the Klavis server catalog and inherits the same ambiguous-empty-success failure mode as the others. No source examined here documents mem0's actual read/write semantics, no task in `mcpmark`/`mcp-atlas`/`Toolathlon-mvp`/`deep-swe` was found exercising a persistent-memory-across-turns mechanism, and no evidence describes memory staleness, conflicting writes, or memory-scope leakage as a distinct failure class. This category is not well established by the gathered evidence.

## 5.14 Partial success / rollback

- Issue #1664 (and its companion open PR #1674) documents that per-provider `normalize_*` response layers — covering Notion, google_calendar, google_docs, linear, mem0, onedrive, and outlook — treat any dict-shaped tool result as a successful vendor object, silently collapsing a structured `{error, type}` envelope (e.g., Notion's real 404/401/403 with `code`/`message`) to `{}`. A maintainer comment confirms this reproduces on `main` across the listed providers. The agent cannot distinguish "succeeded with an empty result" from "failed with a swallowed error" [klavis-klavis-issues, klavis-klavis-prs].
- PR #1521 documents that Strata's Cursor-config writer mutated `~/.cursor/mcp.json` non-atomically and, pre-fix, silently replaced corrupt existing JSON with an empty dict rather than aborting — fixed via backup, atomic temp-file rename, and abort-on-parse-failure [klavis-klavis-prs].
- `deep-swe`'s `koota-entity-snapshot-rollback` task requires implementing `rollbackEntity`/`rollbackWorld` APIs whose correctness depends on exact set-reconciliation (added/removed/changed traits, relations, and entity IDs) against a snapshot, with dozens of narrow edge-case tests (self-referential relations, forward references, ID preservation across a second rollback) — a plausible-but-incomplete implementation passes the happy path while failing the differential grading suite [klavis-deep-swe-repo].
- `mcpmark`'s `transfer_parts` task (5.10 above) is simultaneously partial-success/rollback evidence: `verify_audit_logging` checks that a successful transfer's audit row survives while a failed transfer's audit row is correctly rolled back under standard transaction semantics [klavis-mcpmark-repo].

## 5.15 External-system reconciliation

The Notion/`normalize_*` error-stripping bug (5.14) is itself the central external-system reconciliation failure: real vendor error state (a 404 from Notion's live API) is lost at the tool-normalization boundary before it ever reaches the agent or any downstream grader that inspects returned content [klavis-klavis-issues, klavis-klavis-prs]. `Toolathlon-mvp` documents a second, structurally different reconciliation seam: because its tasks were authored for a monolithic local environment (hardcoded `localhost` ports, local credential file paths), the benchmark runner transparently monkeypatches `socket.getaddrinfo`, `requests`/`aiohttp`, and `builtins.open`/`os.stat` for its own local preprocess/evaluation subprocesses — but explicitly *not* for the agent's own MCP tool calls, which go directly to Klavis-issued URLs unmodified. Any gap in this patched surface silently breaks setup or grading through no action of the agent, and the seam between what the harness redirects and what the agent's actions actually touch is a reconciliation hazard specific to how the benchmark, not the agent, resolves environment identity [klavis-toolathlon-mvp-repo].

## 5.16 Summary of source coverage

| sourceUnitId | Primary contribution |
|---|---|
| klavis-org | Org-level scale/maturity signal; Strata partial-tool-disclosure design; OAuth/credential-persistence issues |
| klavis-klavis-repo | Per-server implementation evidence (Slack bot/user split, Snowflake/Sheets injection PR); informal human-attested acceptance process |
| klavis-klavis-issues | Deep issue-tracker mining: fail-open authorization, silent no-op auth, error-envelope stripping, cross-tool injection amplification |
| klavis-klavis-prs | PR-level fix history: timeout/session corruption, config non-atomicity, process-gated (not correctness-gated) security-fix rejection |
| klavis-mcpmark-repo | External benchmark fork: multi-step ID-propagation tasks, transactional audit-log/rollback tension, async widget-state tasks |
| klavis-mcp-atlas-repo | Scale-AI-attributed benchmark: frozen-snapshot dependency, judge/trajectory decoupling, whole-run retry duplicating side effects |
| klavis-toolathlon-mvp-repo | Third-party benchmark runner: brittle regex verification, decoy-scope discrimination, harness/agent environment-authority seam |
| klavis-deep-swe-repo | Datacurve-attributed coding benchmark: rollback/reconciliation task, concurrency-coalescing task, calibrated difficulty + recorded (non-blocking) scope signals |

## 6. External foundry and benchmark comparison

# 6. External foundry and benchmark comparison

This section reports real investigation results on 15 distinct external projects across 12 investigated source units (some source units bundle more than one project — the program-repair survey covers Defects4J and BugSwarm separately; the open-discovery pass covers METR Task Standard, TheAgentCompany, and AppWorld separately). Every claim below is attributed to its `sourceUnitId`. Where a fetch was partial, gated, or failed outright, that is stated plainly in the entry rather than filled in from assumption — several entries below (MCP-Bench, VOID, the open-discovery trio, and the "terminal-agent-lab" search) carry explicit depth caveats and should be weighted accordingly.

A methodological note before the per-project detail: the task brief for this section warns not to call a static corpus a foundry merely because it contains many tasks. Applying that test strictly changes several classifications from how the source projects describe themselves — most notably METR Task Standard and TheAgentCompany, which the open-discovery narrative summary calls "true foundries," but for which the gathered evidence shows a manually-authored task corpus running against an enforced *schema*, not an automated path from raw source material to generated candidates. Those are reported below as authoring frameworks / runners, not generators, with the reasoning shown.

---

## 6.1 Quick-reference comparison

| Project | sourceUnitId | Repo / pinned state | Classification | Production unit |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CLI-Universe | `foundry-cli-universe` | arXiv:2606.22883v1 (submitted 2026-06-22); no GitHub URL captured | **Foundry** (synthesis engine) | blueprint → Dockerized env → hint-guided solver trajectory |
| TUA-Bench | `foundry-tua-bench` | github.com/facebookresearch/TUA-Bench; arXiv:2606.28480; tuabench.ai | Filtered/curated **benchmark corpus** on the Harbor runner | 1 of 120 tasks in an isolated resettable container, scalar reward |
| Terminal-Bench / Harbor | `foundry-terminal-agent-lab` | github.com/harbor-framework/terminal-bench (moved from laude-institute/terminal-bench) | **Task-authoring framework + runner** (manual task creation, CI-enforced schema) | task directory (instruction.md/task.toml/Dockerfile/solve.sh/test.sh) → numeric reward file |
| MCP-Bench | `foundry-mcp-bench` | github.com/Accenture/mcp-bench; arXiv:2508.20453 — **evidence partial** | **Generator** (synthetic, LLM-authored tasks over real tool chains) | 1 NL task chaining tools across 1–3 of 28 live MCP servers |
| Toolathlon | `foundry-toolathlon` | github.com/hkust-nlp/Toolathlon ("Toolathlon-Verified" release); arXiv:2510.25726; Klavis-AI/Toolathlon-mvp is a downstream sandbox wrapper, not a fork | Manually curated **benchmark corpus + execution verifier suite** | 1 of 108 tasks across 32 real apps, dedicated eval script |

| BugSwarm | `foundry-program-repair-survey` | Tomassi et al., ICSE 2019, arXiv:1903.06725 (PDF read in full) | **Foundry** (CI-log mining) | 1 archived Travis-CI fail→pass build pair, reconstructed in Docker |

| TheAgentCompany | `foundry-open-discovery` | GitHub (NeurIPS 2025 D&B) | **Benchmark corpus + simulated-environment runner** | 1 of 175 tasks, Docker'd real GitLab/Plane/ownCloud/RocketChat + LLM NPCs |
